Re-tuning the *amount* of context each lane gets is deferred to its own issue, gated on retrieval quality rather than on a byte count.

  **What a budget buys, measured.** `benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py` is the producer for every figure here. It packs 300-belief synthetic stores — carrying user locks and speculative-origin beliefs, so the arms this change rewrote for those cases are exercised — under the pre-#1526 cost functions and under the shipped ones, **at the same unchanged budget**, and renders both through each lane's own renderer with compression at its production default of ON. The row below is each lane at the committed replay-soak corpus's 92-character median belief length. Run `uv run python benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py --curve` for the whole length grid; the deviation is not one multiplier and is not quoted as one.

  | Lane | Budget (unchanged) | Emitted bytes, before → after |
  |---|---|---|
  | `hook.DEFAULT_HOOK_TOKEN_BUDGET` (per-turn) | 1500 | 9259 → 6763 |
  | `hook.DEFAULT_SESSION_START_CORE_TOKEN_BUDGET` (`<core>`) | 1500 | 10335 → 5883 |
  | the SessionStart baseline block | 1500 | 1408 → 1408 (see below) |
  | `hook_search_tool.INJECTED_TOKEN_BUDGET` (Grep\|Glob) | 600 | 3305 → 2609 |
  | `hook_search_tool.BASH_INJECTED_TOKEN_BUDGET` | 300 | 1910 → 1678 |
  | `hook_agent_context.INJECTED_TOKEN_BUDGET` | 600 | 4586 → 3114 |
  | `retrieval.DEFAULT_TOKEN_BUDGET` (`aelf search`) | 2400 | 6960 → 6960 |

  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#belief_line_wrapper_chars = 52 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#store_beliefs = 300 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#ups_budget = 1500 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#ups_bytes_before = 9259 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#ups_bytes_after = 6763 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#core_bytes_before = 10335 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#core_bytes_after = 5883 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#session_start_budget = 1500 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#session_start_bytes_before = 1408 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#session_start_bytes_after = 1408 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#search_tool_budget = 600 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#search_tool_bash_budget = 300 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#agent_context_bytes_before = 4586 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#agent_context_bytes_after = 3114 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#cli_search_budget = 2400 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#cli_search_bytes_before = 6960 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#cli_search_bytes_after = 6960 -->

  Two rows in that table are equalities, and neither is a null result. `retrieval.DEFAULT_TOKEN_BUDGET`'s two arms both end on the **L2.5 sub-budget** at this length, not on the budget in the row — the producer names which cap ended each pack rather than reporting a bare byte count, and away from 92 characters this lane moves. the SessionStart baseline block's two arms both end on the **candidate pool**, at every length in the grid, which is the next item. That lane's budget constant is deleted in this release under [#1546](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1546), and the figures above come from a probe budget the benchmark now supplies itself.

  **A seventh renderer, which does not emit `<belief …>` at all ([#1526](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1526)).** `hook_search_tool` emits `[L0] <id-prefix>: <content>` truncated to `PER_LINE_CHAR_CAP = 200`. Charging it the `<belief>` element made a 500-character belief cost 138 tokens for a line that is 200 characters — 51 tokens — long, and left the 23-character `[L0] <id-prefix>: ` prefix charged to nothing. `retrieve()` now takes a `belief_cost_fn`, and that lane passes one built from `_belief_line`, the same function the renderer calls, so the truncation and the escaping are charged because they happened rather than transcribed as a width. Because the old accounting charged *more* than this lane emits past the per-line cap, this is the one lane where the correction changes sign: it emits fewer bytes on short beliefs and more on long ones.

  **What changed, enumerated rather than summarised ([#1526](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1526)).** `retrieval._belief_tokens` — the base cost reached by the L1 tail pack, the L2.5 sub-budget pack, the HRR structural pack and `retrieve()`'s own cost closure — charges the rendered line, including the `speculative="1"` marker ([#1171](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1171)) on the beliefs that carry it. `retrieval.lock_injection_tokens` charges the same line on its verbatim arm and the manifest entry's indent and newline on its [#1016-B](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1016) reference-lock arm. `clustering._belief_tokens`, the default `cost_fn` for the cluster and max-coverage packs, is a deliberate duplicate of the first and moved with it — the claim that it mirrors is now a test rather than a docstring. `retrieve_with_tiers`'s cost closure adds the wrapper to a compressed render through `retrieval._render_wrapper_tokens`, a module-level name so a before/after measurement can reach it. `retrieval._l25_hits` takes the caller's `cost_fn` too, so the L2.5 sub-pack is no longer selected against a line shape the Grep\|Glob lane never emits. And the `<core>` packer costs its own rendered line exactly, by building it, because that line's width genuinely varies: `corr` and `posterior` are interpolated numbers.

  **What deliberately does not change ([#1526](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1526)).** `retrieval.LEGACY_TOKEN_BUDGET` (2000) exists to reproduce the v1.2 caller's budget byte-for-byte on the disabled-entity-index fallback, and re-denominating it would destroy the thing it is for. `retrieval.DEFAULT_L25_TOKEN_SUBBUDGET` stays the literal 400 it has always been. `aelfrice.benchmark.DEFAULT_TOKEN_BUDGET` is a harness constant, not an injection path. And the fixed framing header — 502 characters, 126 tokens, emitted ahead of the first belief by four formatters — is still charged to no budget: reserving it is a separable change, and an uncompensated one at unchanged budgets. How much it would take off each block is not quoted here, because the arm that measured it was reverted along with the reservation and no producer on this tree can re-derive it; the figures go to the follow-up issue, where they describe work not yet done.

  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#framing_header_chars = 502 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#l25_token_subbudget = 400 -->

  **The rebuilder's self-report counts what the block emits ([#1526](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1526)).** `<retrieved-beliefs budget_used="N/M">` was counting belief text rather than rendered lines — a number no reader of the block could check against the block — and now reports what the section emits, so the numerator can exceed the denominator. `context_rebuilder._estimate_belief_tokens` is still on the old currency, and the reason given in an earlier draft — "correcting it would move this block's emitted bytes" — was not true: the block's bytes already move under this change, because `rebuild_v14` takes its non-locked candidates from `retrieve()`. Measured on the producer's stores: 9011 → 9011 bytes at 92 content characters, and 19293 → 16421 at 300. The real reason it is left alone is that `DEFAULT_REBUILDER_TOKEN_BUDGET` **is** reachable from `.aelfrice.toml` as `[rebuilder] token_budget`, unlike `[retrieval] token_budget`, which the hook shadows with an explicit kwarg. Re-denominating the currency a user's configured value is written in would cut their rebuild block without a word; that needs a migration or a currency marker on the key, not a constant edit.

  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#rebuild_bytes_before_92 = 9011 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#rebuild_bytes_after_92 = 9011 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#rebuild_bytes_before_300 = 19293 -->
  <!-- derived: benchmarks/injection_budget_bytes.py#rebuild_bytes_after_300 = 16421 -->

  One knob still does not reach the hook: `[retrieval] token_budget` in `.aelfrice.toml`. The resolver ranks an explicit argument above TOML and the hook always passes one (`aelf search` shadows it the same way, its `--budget` having a default). `docs/user/CONFIG.md` says so at the key.
- **A partial install no longer costs the SessionStart recap ([#1527](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1527)).** Deferring the retrieval subtree took `aelfrice.retrieval` and `aelfrice.hook_search` out of the eager `try` that sets `_IMPORTS_OK`, so an install missing either one, or missing an optional dependency such as `numpy` that only a retrieval call reaches, no longer trips the guard at the top of each lane: the fire runs and the `ImportError` surfaces mid-body, as an unhandled traceback on every fire. `user_prompt_submit`, `pre_compact` and `session_start` now answer it the way the eager guard always has, through one `_report_incomplete_install` helper the guard sites also call — one concise line, exit 0, no traceback. The arm **reports and falls through** rather than returning: `session_start` runs the belief-write recap and the wonder auto-GC after its `try`, neither of which touches retrieval, and returning early would let one absent optional dependency silently delete both.
- **The hook audit log records its own truncation, and the zero-hit fire it was silently dropping ([#1528](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1528)).** `hook_audit.jsonl` is the substrate this project measures itself with — [#1407](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1407) exists so per-fire outcomes are counted rather than inferred — and two properties of the writer made every rate derived from it narrower and more optimistic than it looked. Both defeated that intent the same way: they made an absence indistinguishable from a measurement.

  **Rotation was destructive and silent.** Rollover is an append-then-`os.replace` into a single slot `hook_audit.jsonl.1`, so the second rollover overwrites the first archive and that history is gone. Nothing in the surviving files said so, and a benchmark reading `hook_audit.jsonl*` could not tell a short history from a truncated one. Retention was deliberately **not** widened — N slots multiply the on-disk cost by N on every repo that has an audit log, and the property the issue asks for is detectability, not retention. Each rollover now stamps one `{"hook": "audit_rotation"}` row as the first line of the freshly emptied live file, carrying that file's generation number, a summary of the file just retired (row count and `ts` range), and the one fact nothing else can reconstruct: how many archives have already been discarded.

  **The answer is three-valued, and the third value is what makes it honest.** `audit_window()` reads the markers back as a generation, a `records` count, a first/last `ts`, and a pair of flags: `truncated` when archives are provably gone, `complete` when nothing was discarded and that is provable, and *neither* when the files cannot settle it. The first rollover is `complete` — it fills an empty slot and destroys nothing, and a warning that fired on every rotated log would be no signal at all. A `.1` carrying no generation stamp is the unknown case, and it is the common one: every log already rotated in the wild predates this marker, so one rollover discarded nothing and a second discarded the first archive, and no surviving file can say which. **When such a log next rolls over, the writer reads the doomed `.1`'s existence before the `os.replace` removes it** and stamps `discarded_generations: 1, discarded_unknown: true` — at least one archive destroyed, true count unknowable. Without that read the live file scans as generation 1, and the very rotation that destroyed 10.5 MB of history would have claimed to discard nothing. "Unknown, at least one destroyed" is a truthful answer; "nothing discarded" is not.

  **The zero-hit population was never recorded.** On the `UserPromptSubmit` path the audit write lived inside `if hits:` and `elif gate_skip:`. A fire where retrieval ran and returned zero hits, with the shape gate not firing, matched neither and wrote **no row**. That is exactly the population an analysis wants most — the prompts where retrieval did full work and found nothing — so every hit rate, fires-per-session and cold rate computed from this log had that class missing from its denominator with nothing indicating the gap. Such a fire now writes one row of the same shape with `n_beliefs: 0`, an empty `beliefs[]`, and no `prompt_shape_gate_skip` key, so it is a measured zero rather than a hole and is not confused with the gate-skipped bucket. It carries `sidecar_outcome` and `order_policy` for the same reason the gate-skip branch does: a zero-hit fire can still have paid a full rebuild, and that is the fire [#1380](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1380) is priced on. The zero covers both "BM25 found nothing" and "the project-context, session-exclusion and category filters emptied what it found" — both are a fire that injected nothing, which is what a hit rate's denominator needs; separating them would need a pre-filter count and is out of scope here. The `hook.py` edit is one `else` branch and nothing else, because another change is in flight against that file.

  **`benchmarks/sidecar_rebuild_rate.py` states the window it covered** — first and last row timestamp, whether a `.1` was present, the rotation generation — above the rate. It prints `WINDOW TRUNCATED` with the discarded count when generations are gone, `at least N` when that count is a floor, and `WINDOW UNKNOWN` for a `.1` with no generation stamp. Two ways it could contradict itself are closed: the completeness wording is gated on the generation rather than on a `.1` being among the paths, so a marked live file passed alone no longer prints "no rotation has occurred" under "rotation generation 2"; and an archive handed in without its live file reports unknown rather than complete, because each file records what had been lost when it was *created* and is blind to the loss caused by the rotation that archived it.

  **Compatibility and cost.** Every existing reader filters on a specific `hook` value, so the marker row is inert to all of them and is excluded from the benchmark's row census explicitly. A log with no marker and no `.1` has never rotated and reads as exactly complete; one with a `.1` reads as unknown — and the `.1` counts whenever it is on disk beside the live file, not only when the caller passed it in, because the single-path invocation is the documented one and every log already rotated in the wild is unmarked; `audit_window()` skips missing, unreadable, non-object and unparseable lines rather than raising. Disk is unchanged at two files — a `.2` slot is still never created. The scan runs once per rotation, which is once per `max_bytes` of accumulated log. Measured against a synthetic file at the 10 MiB default cap — 10.00 MiB, 9,939 rows, medians of 7 — it costs **31.2 ms** on the single fire that rotates, so **0.0031 ms** amortised across the 9,939 fires that filled the file, and the marker row is **275 bytes**, 0.0026% of the cap. Read those as one corpus, not as constants: the scan parses every row, so its cost tracks row CONTENT as well as file size, and `[hook_audit] max_bytes` is operator-settable, so a larger cap costs proportionally more per rotation and proportionally less per fire. Quote the ratio rather than the milliseconds for any other cap or corpus. The scan parses every row because `first_ts`/`last_ts` are a true min/max: concurrent hook processes append to this file, so reading the first and last line instead would be an ordering assumption rather than a measurement. A byte-oriented newline count over the same file is roughly an order of magnitude cheaper at **3.7 ms**, but yields only the row count.
- **Six uninstall tests skip, rather than fail, when the optional `[archive]` extra is absent ([#1529](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1529)).** `archive` is an optional extra (`cryptography>=50.0`) and is not in the `dev` dependency group, but three test modules exercised `aelf uninstall --archive` with no guard, so a developer install without the extra failed six tests with `RuntimeError: --archive requires the 'archive' extra`. Measured on a clean sync of `main`, the whole suite went `6 failed, 8547 passed` without the extra and `8553 passed` with it. Because `aelf-pr-open.sh` gates on a local `pytest` run, that blocked opening any pull request, on any subject, until the contributor traced six unrelated uninstall failures back to one missing optional dependency. The guard is `pytest.importorskip("cryptography", reason=...)`, the convention this suite already used at `tests/test_lifecycle.py:259` and `:280` and in five other modules for the `[benchmarks]` extras; the three sibling modules had simply been written without it. A skip can delete coverage as quietly as a failure blocks work, so CI now asserts `import cryptography` immediately after the sync step: that import is the guards' only condition, so proving it holds proves none of the six can skip on a runner. Verified in both directions — without the extra the six report as skips with the reason and nothing fails, and with the extra all 85 tests in those modules run with zero skips.
- **The merge-train reads the whole pull-request body before closing the issues it links ([#1541](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1541)).** Step 7/7 exists because this repo's signed-commit branch protection requires a fast-forward push, and a fast-forward does not trigger GitHub's own auto-close; the step parses `Closes #N` out of the body and closes each match. It piped that body through `head -c 8192` **before** matching, so a keyword written past the 8,192nd byte was truncated away and its issue stayed open. The failure is silent in both directions a reader would check: the merge succeeds, and the train logs `no linked issues parsed from PR body` — the same line a PR that genuinely links nothing produces. Measured on the real bodies of the last four merges, old parse against new: `#1537` (11,823 B, keyword at byte 463) `[1522] → [1522]`; `#1538` (11,242 B, byte 9,148) `[] → [1513]`; `#1539` (9,698 B, byte 8,992) `[] → [1469]`; `#1543` (17,397 B, byte 0) `[1369] → [1369]`. Both bodies over the cut lost their close, and [#1513](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1513) and [#1469](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1469) were closed by hand afterwards. **No input cap replaces the old one.** The cap was the defect: `grep` streams, and the unbounded quantity was never the body but the loop over what came out of it, which is bounded by how many distinct issues one person wrote in one description. Re-adding a larger cap would re-introduce the same bug at a larger offset, so a surprising count now warns on stderr and drops nothing. The parse moves into `scripts/merge_train_linked_issues.py` so it can be tested at all, matching the precedent [#1397](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1397) set for the gate decision; `tests/test_merge_train_linked_issues_1541.py` pins a keyword found at byte 0, 463, 8,172, 8,192, 8,992, 9,148 and 65,536, and carries the old cut as an explicit control so the offsets cannot stop straddling the truncation they were chosen to straddle. Two tests read the workflow itself, with `#` comment lines stripped — the comment explaining the defect necessarily quotes the pipeline that caused it, and a naive substring search finds the bug in its own postmortem. Five mutations were run and all five go red: truncating the parse again, dropping the regex's leading `\b`, letting the keyword match without whitespace, silently capping the output, and re-adding the truncating pipe to the workflow. **Two fidelity gaps are documented and deliberately left.** The keyword set is `closes` / `fixes` / `resolves`, narrower than the nine GitHub acts on, so `Fixed #N` does not close here though a merge commit would have closed it; and fenced code blocks are not excluded, so a body demonstrating the syntax closes the issue it names. One misses a close and the other makes a wrong one. Both change which issues close rather than whether the body is read, which is why neither is taken here.
- **A belief shown in the session-start sub-block is no longer shown again below it in the same envelope ([#1547](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1547)).** `_format_hits_with_session_start` builds one `<aelfrice-memory>` envelope out of two halves — the embedded session-start sub-block, with its `<locked>` and `<core>` sections, and the per-turn retrieval hits — and nothing stopped the same belief appearing in both. Measured over 1,127 live `UserPromptSubmit` rows: **1,589 redundant `<belief>` elements**, **164 of 1,124 rows (14.6%)** carrying at least one, and the pairings concentrated in `<core>` against a per-turn hit (829) and `<locked>` against a per-turn hit (740), with 13 more against `<retrieved-beliefs>` and 2 appearing three times. The duplicate copies were **729,527 of 17,179,106 characters, 4.25%** of everything those rows emitted. The sub-block's own ids now count as already rendered for the hits below them, so a repeat renders through the existing `_renders_as_manifest` path as a `seen <id>: "<topic>"` pointer rather than a second full copy. No new file, no lock, no flag, no turn key: both halves are written by one call from one frame, so the ids are simply read off the block that is already in hand. **This recovers bytes, not budget** — the pack loop has spent its budget before anything renders, so the freed space admits no further belief; the envelope gets shorter and the tail does not grow. The id scan deliberately matches `id="[^"]+"` rather than a hex class, because live stores carry two id forms — 16-character hex and 26-character ULID — and a hex-only pattern silently skips the ULIDs, which sit on the largest elements in the corpus; four independent readers of this code made exactly that mistake before the comment at the pattern existed, and `test_a_ulid_id_dedupes_too` is the guard. `tests/test_envelope_dedupe_1547.py` holds fourteen tests, and the one that matters most is the neutrality contract: a store whose sub-block shares no id with its hits emits a **byte-identical** block, which is what stops this being a cut rather than a de-duplication. Four mutations were run and all four go red: removing the union (8 failed), narrowing the pattern to hex (4 failed), replacing the union with an assignment so the [#1382](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1382) ledger set is discarded (1 failed), and dropping the duplicate silently instead of leaving a pointer (5 failed). The tree was verified clean after each revert.
- **The merge-train's auto-close now asks GitHub which issues a pull-request body closes, instead of emulating GitHub's Markdown reader ([#1549](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1549)).** Step 7/7 exists because this repo's signed-commit branch protection requires a fast-forward push, and a fast-forward does not trigger GitHub's own auto-close. [#1541](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1541) made the whole body reach the matcher; #1549 ruled that the step must read that body the way GitHub reads it. The first attempt at the ruling emulated the reading: a line-state scanner classified every line of the body as prose, fence, indent, quote or comment, and fired a keyword only in prose. **That approach is deleted.** Five review rounds each fixed one reading and found another block type, the last of them a wrong close on this repository's own pull-request template — a body with `## Linked issues` followed by an indented `Fixed #7` linked #7, while GitHub renders it inside `<pre><code>` and closes nothing. The same round found the scanner wrong after a setext underline, a thematic break, a GFM table row, an ATX heading and a block quote whose last block is not a paragraph. The surface being emulated was CommonMark's whole block grammar, so 172 lines of it — `_FENCE_RE` through `_reason_at`, the four scanning functions and the five line-state constants — went, rather than growing by one case per round.

  **What `scripts/merge_train_linked_issues.py` does now.** It renders the body through GitHub's own renderer, `gh api --method POST /markdown` with `{"mode": "gfm", "context": "OWNER/NAME", "text": <body>}` on stdin, and acts on the issue-link anchors GitHub itself produced. Text inside a fenced code block, an indented code block, an inline code span or an HTML comment never produces an anchor, so every block-classification question disappears at once instead of one per review round. GitHub anchors every reference and not only closing ones, so which anchor is a *directive* is decided by adjacency in the rendered text: for each anchor, the text immediately before it in the same block must end in one of the nine keywords — `close`, `closes`, `closed`, `fix`, `fixes`, `fixed`, `resolve`, `resolves`, `resolved`, in any case — optionally followed by a colon bound to the keyword and any whitespace. Adjacency is per block, so a paragraph, a heading, a table cell and a list item each stand alone; an anchor ends the run, which is GitHub's one-keyword-per-issue rule; and a keyword written inside a code span does not arm the anchor beside it.

  **The failure policy is loud, and closes nothing.** An unreachable renderer, a non-zero `gh` exit and a 200 carrying nothing all raise `RendererUnavailable`; `main` then prints two `error:` lines on stderr, prints **nothing** on stdout, and exits 2. Two alternatives were rejected in the module docstring rather than left implicit: falling back to a local parse is the emulation this change deletes and would make its wrong closes exactly when the renderer that would have caught them is down; and treating an unreachable renderer as "no links" is the silent no-op #1549 exists to kill, since the step would print what a body with no keyword prints. Closing nothing is recoverable by a human reading the step log; a wrong close is not. There is no retry, because this step runs after the merge has landed. `merge-train.yml` turns the non-zero exit into a `::error::` annotation naming the PR, replacing the `|| true` that used to swallow it, and the step log now has three distinct outcomes rather than two: `linked issues UNDETERMINED`, `no linked issues parsed from PR body`, and the close loop. Driving the extracted fragment under the step's own `set -euo pipefail` with a stub tool prints each of the three.

  **The renderer call is an injected seam, and the seam itself is tested.** `render_markdown(body, repo, *, run=None)` resolves `subprocess.run` in the function body rather than in the signature — a default argument is evaluated once, at import, so the signature form would bind the real runner past any replacement of it, and a suite that looked injected would reach GitHub on every run. That is not hypothetical: `test_the_render_seam_is_late_bound` failed against the live endpoint before the default moved. Tests pin the call twice. `test_the_real_call_is_pinned_argument_by_argument` injects the runner and asserts the exact argv, the exact JSON payload, `capture_output`, `text`, `encoding`, `check` and the timeout. `test_the_shipped_command_runs_gh_with_the_pinned_arguments` puts a `gh` of the tests' own on `PATH`, runs the shipped CLI end to end and reads back the argv and stdin that `gh` actually received. What GitHub renders is replayed from `tests/data/merge_train_github_renders.json`, seven verbatim responses from the real endpoint; `scripts/record_merge_train_renders.py --dry-run` re-sends every body and reports any GitHub now answers differently, and reports `0 of 7 records drifted` on this tree. Running both suites with a poison `gh` first on `PATH` — one that exits 97 and prints `POISONED` — still reports `233 passed`, so no test depends on the ambient `gh` or on the network.

  **The workflow needs no new permission, and this was measured rather than assumed.** `POST /markdown` against this public repository answers HTTP 200 with the issue-link anchors for an entirely unauthenticated request: `curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' -X POST --data '{"mode":"gfm","context":"robotrocketscience/aelfrice","text":"Closes #7"}' https://api.github.com/markdown` prints `200`. The step already exports `GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}` and the job already declares `contents: write`, so nothing was added; the only workflow changes are the invocation, the annotation and the comments. Body size is not a constraint below the renderer's own cap — a 264,096-character body renders and links all three of its issues, checked with the `gh api --method POST /markdown --input big.json` command recorded in the module docstring; the cap itself is the next paragraph. Diagnostics still go to stderr only, because command substitution captures stdout and the shell splits it into the close loop.

  **The renderer has an input cap of its own, and on this step that has to be said out loud.** `POST /markdown` answers HTTP 403 `too_large` above 400 KB; a 420,000-character body reproduces it. That is a reintroduced input bound on the step whose defect *was* an input bound, so the entry says so rather than leaving a reader to find it: unlike `head -c 8192` it is loud — `gh` exits non-zero, `RendererUnavailable` is raised, and the failure policy above applies, so the step closes nothing and annotates. Can a pull-request body reach it? Not through the text. GitHub caps a body at 65,536 characters — widely reported as the 422 `Body is too long (maximum is 65536 characters)` and **UNVERIFIED here**, since measuring it would mean creating a pull request — which is at most 262,144 UTF-8 bytes. But it could reach the cap through the tool's own encoding, and did: `json.dumps` escapes an astral character to twelve ASCII bytes by default, so a legal 65,536-character emoji body became a 786,501-byte request and was refused, while the same body unescaped is 262,213 bytes and renders all three of its issues. `render_markdown` now passes `ensure_ascii=False`, which puts the cap beyond the reach of any body GitHub would accept, and names `encoding="utf-8"` on the call rather than inheriting the runner's locale, since the payload is no longer pure ASCII. `RENDER_LIMIT_BYTES` and `MAX_BODY_CHARACTERS` record both numbers so `test_the_largest_body_github_accepts_fits_inside_the_renderers_cap` asserts the relation instead of a reader trusting a paragraph.

  **The render says a reference exists; it does not say which object the reference named.** Review round six found a live wrong close. GitHub rewrites `Closes https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/discussions/1549` into `<a class="issue-link" data-url=".../issues/1549">#1549</a>` — byte-for-byte what a plain `#1549` produces — so the shipped CLI printed `1549`, exit 0, empty stderr, and the train would have run `gh issue close 1549` on an unrelated issue. Discussions are numbered independently of issues and this repository has them enabled (`gh api repos/robotrocketscience/aelfrice --jq '.has_discussions'` prints `true`). The check cannot live on the render, which has already rewritten `data-url`, so `DISCUSSION_RE` scans the *source* body and `parse` hands the `(owner, repo, number)` triples to `close_directives`, which refuses any anchor resolving to one of them. The rule is deliberately blunt — a body writing both spellings for one number loses the close and gets a warning — because the module's standing asymmetry says a missed close is an open issue a human sees. **Every other URL shape was audited against the live renderer**, one `POST /markdown` per row, and each is ruled on in the docstring and replayed from a new `urlforms` record: `/issues/N` closes N and `/pull/N` is refused (the two share one number space, which is what makes the reference render, not what makes it close — see round eight below); `/issues/N#issuecomment-...` closes N and `/pull/N#discussion_r...` is refused, the fragment naming a comment *on* N either way; `/discussions/N` is refused; `/pull/N/files`, `/commit/SHA`, `https://github.com/orgs/ORG/discussions/N`, `/milestone/N`, `/projects/N`, `/releases/tag/...`, `/blob/...` and `/wiki/...` render ordinary links and are not references at all.

  **Two of the module's own rulings refused in silence, which is the one outcome AC5 forbids.** `Closes : #1549` and `Closes::#5` produced no stdout, no stderr and exit 0 — byte-for-byte what a body with no keyword produces, so the step logged `no linked issues parsed from PR body` and moved on, while the module docstring claimed every keyword found and not acted on prints a warning. `DECLINED_RE` now recognises an anchor whose preceding text ends in a keyword followed by nothing but colons and whitespace that `ADJACENT_RE` declined, and each gets its own `warning:` line with `DECLINED_SEPARATOR` as the reason. A form GitHub itself does not act on is still silent, because there is no close directive in it to report: `precloses #7`, `Closes issue #8` and `Closes#6` warn about nothing, and pinning that line is what keeps the warnings worth reading.

  **The adjacency window was an untested tuning constant that could invent a close.** `_TAIL` bounds how much text before an anchor the rule sees, and its comment invited a trim ("the rest is slack"). The window is a *suffix*, so `(?:^|\W)` could read the cut as the start of a block: at a six-character window a paragraph ending `... the last one is prefixed #7` closes #7, because `fixed` lands at position 0 and `^` stands in for the word boundary `prefixed` was supposed to defeat. The flip point is `len(keyword)` plus the trailing whitespace, so it differs per keyword — 6 for `prefixed`, 9 for `unresolved` — and `/usr/share/dict/words` holds 58 distinct words ending in one of the nine. A clipped window now refuses `^` and demands a real non-word character, which makes every match on the window a match on the whole run: clipping can lose a close, never invent one, and `_TAIL` is a cost bound rather than a ruling. Both directions are pinned — a sweep of every window size from 1 to 64 over six such words, a control that a real directive survives every window the bound allows, and the shipped value asserted against both a derived lower bound and a literal. The same pass found that the clipped flag had to come *back down* at a block boundary: left set, it makes the rule demand a non-word character that a block-opening `Closes #7` cannot have, and the close is lost in silence.

  **Two branches the review called dead, resolved one each way.** The reset on a non-issue-link `<a>` start tag is not dead, but its comment named the wrong case: a well-formed `[see](url)` is ended by `handle_endtag` anyway, and what the reset is actually for is an **unterminated** `<a>`, which `html.parser` never closes — without it `Closes <a href=...>` followed by an issue anchor arms that anchor. The comment now says so and the case is pinned. The `if tag != "br"` guard in `handle_startendtag` genuinely could not change any outcome — `br` is inline and is not `a`, so the synthetic end tag resets nothing — and is deleted; what remains is pinned on the self-closing spelling that is the only input reaching that handler, including a self-closing anchor that only the end-tag half records.

  **Two behaviours changed, both deliberately.** The tool now carries repository identity, because it must send a render `context` and GitHub returns a `data-url` on every anchor, so `--repo OWNER/NAME` (or `GITHUB_REPOSITORY`) is required and missing it is exit 2 rather than a guess. An anchor resolving to this repository is closed however it was spelled — `#N`, `GH-N`, `OWNER/NAME#N`, or a full `https://github.com/OWNER/NAME/issues/N` — because GitHub renders all four identically and closes all four. The emulating revision refused the last two indiscriminately, its own spellings included, only because it could not tell its own name from another's; that refusal is gone, and a link to a *different* repository is still refused out loud. A reference GitHub declines to render at all — `Fixes octo-org/octo-repo#100`, for a repository GitHub cannot resolve — leaves no anchor to hang a reason on, so a source-level scan reports it as `not linked by GitHub's renderer`. That scan is diagnostics only; it never decides a close, and it is not claimed to be a superset of what does, since `Closes *#7*` renders an armed anchor whose source the flat regex does not match. **Four divergences from GitHub remain and are stated in the module docstring**: commit messages are not read; cross-repository links are refused rather than followed; the pull request's target branch is not checked, since the train only ever fast-forwards `main`; and a block quote is refused although adjacency alone would close it — an anchor inside `<blockquote>` renders exactly like one in a paragraph, and refusing it is a ruling, made because a body quoting an earlier review comment that says `Fixes #N` is a real wrong close. That last one is an exact test on the rendered tree rather than a grammar to emulate, and dropping the `in_quote` branch from `close_directives` reverses it in one line. The three colon spacings — `Closes:#10` links, `Closes : #10` and `Closes::#10` do not — are rulings for the same reason: GitHub renders an anchor for all three and publishes no close processor.

  **A legal repository name aborted every close, and that is round seven's blocking defect.** `close_directives` raises `RendererUnavailable` for an issue-link anchor whose `data-url` it cannot read, and the failure policy above turns that raise into nothing closed for the *whole body*. So the pattern reading those URLs was the one place where being too strict aborts the step — and it was too strict: it spelled a repository name as "alphanumeric at each end", which is this module's invention. GitHub reserves no position, documenting only that "The repository name must not exceed 100 characters, and can only contain ASCII letters, digits, and the characters `.`, `-`, and `_`". `.github` is such a name and a common one, so a body writing `Closes github/.github#5` beside `Closes #1549` rendered two anchors, the first carrying `data-url="https://github.com/github/.github/issues/5"`, and the shipped CLI answered `rc=2` on an empty stdout with `error: an issue-link anchor carried an unreadable URL` on stderr — closing neither, its own repository's issue included. The docstring's own ruling for an anchor resolving elsewhere is `CROSS_REPO`, one `warning:` line with the rest of the body still closing, and that is what it does now. The repair is not a wider name grammar but **no name grammar at all on that side**: `_ISSUE_HREF_RE` reads the owner and the name as whole path segments, which cannot fail for anything GitHub can put there, because a path segment never contains a `/`. Width decides nothing on its own, since those segments are only ever compared against `--repo`. Every other shape the review raised was settled against the live renderer rather than argued: a leading dot in the *owner* cannot reach `data-url` at all, because GitHub anchors `.github/.github#5` as `github/.github` and leaves the dot outside the anchor; a name at the documented 100-character maximum is read, and no length is written into the pattern because a length bound is one more way to abort; `https://github.com/github/%2Egithub/issues/5` renders no anchor, and were a percent-encoded spelling of *this* repository ever to arrive, nothing here decodes it, so it compares unequal and is refused — a missed close rather than a wrong one; a trailing slash and a query string are both accepted, though GitHub normalises `data-url` to the bare `/issues/N` either way. What still raises is a `data-url` that is not a github.com issue or pull URL, which is the output-shape change the raise was always for. The source-level scans keep a name grammar, widened to GitHub's documented character set and ended by the literal that must follow — `#`, `/issues/`, `/pull/` or `/discussions/`. That is not only a diagnostic question: `DISCUSSION_RE` decides a refusal, so a repository whose name the grammar could not spell had no protection from the discussions wrong close at all.

  **The case fold that makes the discussions guard work was unpinned on the side that matters.** GitHub canonicalises `data-url` to lower case and leaves the body as the author typed it, so `Closes https://github.com/RobotRocketScience/AelfRice/discussions/21` arrives as a lower-cased anchor and a mixed-case source, and only `discussion_targets`' fold closes the gap — the anchor-side fold is a no-op there. Deleting it left the whole suite green and re-opened exactly the round-six wrong close. Folding in two places is what allowed that, so it now happens once, in `_identity`, and both callers go through it. A new `identity` record holds GitHub's verbatim render of a body that writes the mixed-case discussions URL, the mixed-case `RobotRocketScience/AelfRice#22` that must still close, and the dotted `github/.github#5` above, so both halves are replayed against GitHub's own bytes rather than against a hand-written anchor.

  **Three constants were asserted only against themselves.** `_INLINE_TAGS` lists the 24 tags that do not end a run of text, and only `a`, `br` and the start tag of `em` were exercised anywhere — 20 of the 24 could be deleted with the suite green, while `**Closes** #7` renders `<strong>Closes</strong>` and, with `strong` gone, loses its close in *silence*: no keyword found, so no warning either, which is the one outcome AC5 forbids. There is now one row per member with the tag wrapped around the keyword, and the list they are drawn from is written out in the test rather than read from the module — written derived first, and dropping `strong` then still left 216 passed. `a` is excluded on purpose, because an anchor *ends* a run and `<a>Closes</a>` arms nothing; what its membership decides is whether any anchor is opened at all, and dropping it fails 122 tests. `RENDER_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` was read back from itself by both of its assertions, so 30 could become 1 with the suite green while every render slower than a second aborted the close of every issue in the body — on exactly the loaded runner where a render is slow. It is now bracketed from two sources that are not the constant: 17 live renders measured on 2026-09-15 over three body sizes ran between 0.37s and 1.03s, the slowest on the largest body this module can send, and the workflow's own `timeout-minutes` is read out of `merge-train.yml`. `RENDER_LIMIT_BYTES` and `MAX_BODY_CHARACTERS` are read by no production path — deliberately, since an input bound was #1541's defect — and were bounded from one side only, so the cap could be widened to 4 MB while the docstring beside it still published 400 KB. They are kept rather than deleted, because the docstring's argument is a *relation* between two measured numbers and a relation left in prose rots unwatched; both halves are now asserted, the escaped payload for the largest acceptable body exceeding the cap (which is what makes `ensure_ascii=False` load-bearing) and the unescaped one falling under it, and each figure is re-read out of the docstring prose rather than out of its own definition.

  **Twenty-one further mutations were run for round seven, and all twenty-one go red.** These are measured against the 217-passed baseline this round leaves, by the same method as the campaign above — one exact string replacement, reverted by rewriting the file from a saved copy of its original bytes, with `__pycache__` cleared between runs. Read GitHub's URL through a name grammar again (9), forbid a leading dot in the source-side name grammar (1), stop folding the owner in the one place both sides share (5), stop folding the name there (5), let the source scan build its triples unfolded again (4), let the anchor scan build its triple unfolded again (1), drop `strong` from the inline set (2), drop `img` (2), drop `g-emoji` (2), keep only the three inline tags anything exercised (22), drop `a` from the inline set (122), trim the render timeout to one second (1), stretch it past the job bound (1), widen the renderer cap to 4 MB (2), narrow it below a legal body (2), drift the body cap from the figure the docstring publishes (2), re-add a local input bound at the renderer's cap (1), publish a renderer cap the constant does not carry (1), drop the abort ruling from the docstring (1), drop the case-fold ruling (1), skip an anchor whose URL cannot be read instead of raising (4). One mutation survived the first run of this campaign and it was a weak assertion rather than a defect: the inline-tag rows were parametrised over `_INLINE_TAGS` itself, so deleting a member deleted its row, and dropping `strong` from it left 216 passed. The list is spelled out in the test now and the mutation fails two rows.

  **Tests deleted, and why.** Every case that pinned the line-state scanner went with it: the fence-character and fence-length rules, the info-string rule, the unterminated-fence and unterminated-comment rules, the code-span-versus-comment ordering pair, the unmatched-backtick rule, the half-open-span end-offset rule, the eleven-row sweep of what may precede an indented code block, and the per-reason assertions that named `IN_FENCE`, `IN_INDENT`, `IN_SPAN` and `IN_COMMENT`. None of them describes code that still exists. What they were protecting — that a keyword GitHub reads as code closes nothing, and that the refusal is never silent — is pinned harder now, against GitHub's own recorded output rather than against a parser agreeing with itself. `tests/test_merge_train_linked_issues_1541.py` kept its offset cases and its truncation control and handed the question of which *forms* link to the #1549 module, since that is no longer a property of a regex this repo owns.

  **Sixty-eight mutations were run against the two suites and all sixty-eight go red.** Every count is re-derived on this tree, against the 168-passed baseline that `uv run pytest tests/test_merge_train_close_keywords_1549.py tests/test_merge_train_linked_issues_1541.py -q` reported at commit 7fa1fe37, before review round seven added 49 collected tests and moved the baseline to 217; no figure is carried over from the forty-nine-mutation campaign this paragraph replaces, because the code under several of them changed. Each mutation is a single exact string replacement, reverted by rewriting the file from a saved copy of its original bytes — never `git checkout`, which would destroy uncommitted work — with `__pycache__` cleared between runs so a stale `.pyc` could not invert a result. failure-policy section from the docstring (1), drop the divergence list (1), drop the reference-form enumeration (1), drop GitHub's keyword-page citation (1), no longer name the injection seam (1), revert the docstring to the emulation framing (1), silence the tool's stderr in the workflow (1), swallow a failed determination with || true (1), no longer pass the repository to the tool (2), collapse UNDETERMINED into the no-keyword message (1), drop every live 2>/dev/null the vacuity control reads (1), drop the live 2>&1 the vacuity control reads (1), re-add the head -c 8192 cut (1).

  **Five mutations survived the first run of that campaign, and three were real defects rather than weak assertions.** Leaving the clipped-window flag set across a block boundary changed nothing the suite could see, yet it silently loses a close from any body whose directive opens a block after a long one; the flag is now pinned by `test_a_long_block_does_not_poison_the_block_after_it`. Two docstring checks read the whole file, so they passed on a docstring that no longer said any of it — the same words appear in the comments on `RENDER_LIMIT_BYTES` and `MAX_BODY_CHARACTERS` — and they now read `__doc__` and assert the section heading. The other two were mis-specified mutations, not survivors: one left the `return 2` it was supposed to remove, and one dropped a single `2>/dev/null` from a workflow that has two. Both were rewritten and go red. Separately, one mutation survived the campaign before this one and the code changed rather than the count: making `<br>` contribute a newline to the run was a verified no-op, because adjacency already allows zero whitespace and GitHub emits a literal newline beside the tag. That branch was deleted rather than decorated with a test, and what remains load-bearing — that `<br>` does not *end* the run — is pinned on HTML written without that newline and fails under `let a line break end the run of text`. Per the issue, `CONTRIBUTING.md` gains nothing: enumerating accepted spellings is what the other framing would have required.

  **A pull-request reference closed something GitHub never closes, and an earlier ruling on this branch had it backwards — round eight's first blocking defect.** GitHub's linked-issue mechanism links *issues*. A body writing `Closes https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/pull/1504` renders `<a class="issue-link" data-url=".../issues/1504" data-hovercard-type="pull_request">`, the module read only `data-url`, and the shipped CLI printed `1504`. The train would then have carried the wrong close out rather than skipping it: step 7/7 checks idempotency with `gh issue view 1504 --json state`, which resolves the pull request and answers `MERGED` rather than `CLOSED`, so the skip does not fire and `gh issue close 1504` runs. `#1504`, `GH-1504` and `robotrocketscience/aelfrice#1504` all reach the anchor the same way. The docstring had ruled the opposite — that a `/pull/N` URL "names the same object GitHub would close", because pull requests and issues share one number space. They do share it; that is what makes the reference *render*, not what makes it close. The ruling is corrected in place, quoting the sentence it reverses, rather than edited away. **What decides now** is `data-hovercard-type`, the one attribute in the render that says which object an anchor names: `issue` for an issue and `pull_request` for a pull request, in every spelling, measured against the live endpoint on 2026-09-15 with one `POST /markdown` per row and recorded as a new `kinds` record. Only `issue` is acted on; anything else is refused out loud, and the warning names the label GitHub gave it, so a label GitHub has not emitted yet reads as itself rather than as "pull request". The repository check stays in front of it, so `cli/cli#1` — a pull request *and* another repository — still reads as `CROSS_REPO`, which is the more useful sentence in the step log. **When the attribute is absent** the anchor is refused, and refused rather than raised: a raise costs every close in the body and prints the step's `::error::` telling a human to close the linked issues by hand, which is an invitation to make exactly the wrong close; a refusal costs one open issue and one `warning:` line. It is on all 32 issue-link anchors in the recorded corpus, so it is not a shape GitHub emits today.

  **Every closing reference in the recorded corpus named a pull request, so the fixtures had to be re-recorded.** The bodies used small numbers, and in this repository 1, 2, 3, 7, 10, 13, 21 and 22 are all pull requests, as is the `github/.github#5` the `identity` record names — 26 of the 27 anchors in the file came back labelled `pull_request`. Once the label is read, that corpus can only demonstrate refusals: no record would close anything at all, and the control tests that prove a table row and a heading *do* close would have nothing left to assert. Each reference meant to close is now a real issue of this repository, while a pull request is kept exactly where the refusal is the point — `urlforms` still writes `/pull/1504`. Nothing else moved: the block structure, the separators and the URL shapes are unchanged, and the numbers that render no anchor keep the small values they had. `python3 scripts/record_merge_train_renders.py --dry-run` reports `0 of 7 records drifted` on this tree.

  **A body that is only HTML comments aborted the step — round eight's second blocking defect.** `<!-- Closes #1549 -->` renders as the empty string with `gh` exiting 0. `render_markdown`'s `if not proc.stdout.strip()` raised, `main` returned 2, and the workflow printed `::error::merge-train could not determine the linked issues for PR #N; nothing was closed. Close them by hand.` — an invitation to make the wrong close, for a body whose GitHub answer is "nothing is linked". A pull-request template stripped to its guidance comments, or a body that is only a bot marker, is a realistic body. An empty document is now returned: no anchors, no close, exit 0. The keyword inside the comment is not silent — the source scan reports it as `NOT_LINKED`, which is true of it, and that is the property the raise was defending. **The raise is kept for the cases it can still tell apart** — a non-zero exit, an unrunnable `gh` and a timeout — and it cannot be kept for the exit-0 case, which the entry says rather than leaving the raise's disappearance unexplained: an empty document is byte-for-byte the same answer from a comment-only body and from a broken `gh`, so no rule on the transport separates them, and deciding from the *body* would mean knowing which bodies must render — the CommonMark block grammar this change deleted. Measured on 2026-09-15, three constructs come back as rc 0 and zero bytes: an HTML comment block, a link reference definition (`[a]: https://example.com`) and a footnote definition (`[^fn]: a footnote`). What the empty case gets instead of a raise is a `note:` line naming it, so the log tells "rendered nothing" apart from "rendered a document with no directive", and a renderer that answers nothing produces a missed close that every keyword in the body reports rather than an abort that asks for a hand-close.

  **Thirteen mutations were run for round eight, and all thirteen go red.** Measured against the `233 passed` baseline this round leaves, by the same method as the campaigns above — one exact string replacement, reverted by rewriting the file from a saved copy of its original bytes, never `git checkout`, with `__pycache__` cleared on both sides of every run. Close every anchor whatever GitHub labelled it (7), close an anchor GitHub did not label (2), raise on an unlabelled anchor instead of refusing (8), put the label check ahead of the repository check (4), name every non-issue label `pull_request` (1), drop the label from one recorded anchor (4), default the tests' own anchor helper to no label (17), raise again on an empty document (4), return the empty document with no note (2), print the note for every render (4), drop the source scan so an empty render is silent (17), drop the corrected pull-request ruling from the docstring (1), drop the empty-document ruling from the docstring (1). **Two of those survived the first run, and both were weak assertions rather than defects.** The two docstring guards matched a phrase that three other bullets cross-reference by name, so renaming the section that argues a ruling left the phrase in the file and both mutations reported `233 passed`; they now match the heading line. Both suites still report `233 passed` with a poisoned `gh` first on `PATH` — one that exits 97 and prints `POISONED` — and the poison was verified rather than assumed: `which gh` resolves to it and `gh --version` exits 97, and `record_merge_train_renders.py --dry-run`, which does use the real endpoint, fails under the same `PATH` with `gh exited 97`.
- **The injected memory block is bounded again, and user locks are exempt from the bound ([#1551](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1551)).** `DEFAULT_HOOK_TOKEN_BUDGET = 1500` is passed to `retrieve()` and the packers in `clustering.py` honour it, but `<locked>`, `<core>`, `<recent-work>` and the retrieval hits are packed independently and concatenated, so no budget bounded the block that reached the model. `_write_hook_audit_record` already measured the result with the same 4-chars-per-token estimator the budgeter uses and wrote the overrun to `hook_audit.jsonl`; nothing acted on it.

  **One write path.** Every `<aelfrice-memory>` write now goes through `_write_memory_block`, which trims, notes and emits. Three sites emit that envelope — `user_prompt_submit`'s retrieval branch, its `elif gate_skip:` branch, and `session_start` — and only the first was ever bounded. The gate-skip branch ships a session's whole `<locked>` and `<core>` sub-block whenever the #674 prompt-shape gate refuses BM25 on a first prompt (a first prompt under 12 characters, an acknowledgement), which on shipped defaults is routine: 17,201 estimated tokens from 300 user locks of 159 characters — `"lockword "` plus 150 characters of padding — against a 6,000-token ceiling, with nothing on stderr. Re-derive with `uv run python scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --gate-skip`, which seeds that fixture and disables the ceiling; `--emit-figures` publishes the same number so CI re-runs it.
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#gate_skip_tokens_300_locks_150 = 17201 -->

  **The ceiling bounds that envelope, not the whole payload.** `user_prompt_submit` also writes a `<cadence-checkpoint>` block, from its own `sout.write` above the memory block, and that block carries the rebuilder's budget rather than this one. Neither bounds their sum, so a cadence-enabled store can exceed `HOOK_BLOCK_TOKEN_CEILING` across the payload while the memory block itself is inside it. That is filed as [#1560](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1560) rather than folded in here: the shed order below removes prompt-independent lanes before the prompt's own hits, so extending it over the cadence block would make the hook drop retrieved beliefs to make room for a rebuild recap, and that trade has not been measured. `[cadence] enabled` is unset by default, so the second block is absent on a stock install.

  **The drop policy: both bounds stop at `lock="user"`.** `enforce_block_ceiling` drops whole `<belief>` elements until the body fits `HOOK_BLOCK_TOKEN_CEILING`, and never drops a user-locked one; `_cap_belief_content` truncates one belief's content at `BELIEF_CONTENT_CHAR_CAP = 1200` at each render site — the retrieval hits, `<core>`, and the non-locked entries of `<locked>` — and never truncates user-locked content. That is the #379 / #1016-B contract: locks are the always-injected pool, and a bound that deleted them would have been the thing that broke it. An earlier revision of this change did exactly that — on a 300-lock store all 300 locked elements were removed, leaving an empty `<locked>` section under 300 dangling `seen <id>` manifest pointers. A block that cannot fit without dropping a lock is emitted over the ceiling with a one-line note on stderr naming the overrun and saying that no further span was droppable. That note used to prescribe `aelf lock --reference`; the advice was withdrawn here after it was measured, and restored by [#1558](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1558) in this same release once the render gap it depended on was closed. What was measured: `_build_session_start_subblock`'s `<locked>` loop rendered every lock verbatim with no `is_reference_lock` branch, so on a session's **first** prompt the tier changed nothing. On one 30,026-character lock the gate-skip branch emitted 7700 estimated tokens and the retrieval branch 7796 — identically frozen and reference, the retrieval branch carrying the full text *and* a `ref` pointer to it in the same envelope. From the second prompt the sub-block is gone, `_split_belief_lines` renders the locks and the tier did bound: 256 tokens against 7683. `session_start`'s own baseline path honoured it too (233 against 7660). The whole table is `scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --reference-tier`, whose module constant is the fixture; every manifest-bearing figure depends on the lock's content, not only its length. #1558's entry carries the remedy the note now names and the re-derived first-prompt columns. The only spans ever removed are complete elements and the `seen` pointers that name them — the paragraph below is why the pointer goes with its element — so every framing tag, including the `<aelfrice-locks-manifest>` wrapper, is untouched and the block stays well-formed, and the ids of anything dropped are returned so the audit record, the `injection_events` rows, the session ring, `belief_touches` and the #1382 ledger credit only what was actually emitted.
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_gate_skip_first_frozen = 7700 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_retrieval_first_frozen = 7796 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_turn_two_reference = 256 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_turn_two_frozen = 7683 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_session_start_reference = 233 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_session_start_frozen = 7660 -->

  **The shed order is by lane, so the prompt's own hits go last.** The lanes are emitted `<locked>`, `<core>`, `<recent-work>`, per-turn hits, so a dropper that took the end of the body took the retrieval hits *this prompt* selected and kept the `<core>` pool the prompt had no part in choosing — `<core>` ranks by corroboration and posterior, `<recent-work>` by the git state of the checkout, and neither consults the prompt. The removal order is now `<core>` tail-first, then `<recent-work>`, then the hits, with locks undroppable throughout; within a lane it is still tail-first, because each lane is already sorted worst-last. Measured on 50 user locks of 150 characters, 20 `<core>` beliefs without the query term and 20 hits with it — re-derive with `uv run python scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --lanes` — the block carried 6/20 hits and 19/20 core untrimmed, 0/20 and 17/20 under the old order, and 6/20 and 7/20 under the new one: every hit the untrimmed block had, and the bytes taken out of the lane the prompt did not choose.

  **A dropped element takes its `seen` pointer with it.** The dangling pointers above were an instance of a wider class, not a locks-only accident: any dropped element that another line points at leaves the pointer behind. `retrieval.seen_manifest_line` states the contract — the entry exists because "the full text is already in this context window, above" — so a `seen <id>` line whose element the trim deleted tells the model to look up text that is not in the block. On a session's first prompt #1547's dedupe puts both halves in one envelope, rendering a belief verbatim in `<core>` and as a pointer among the per-turn hits, and a `<core>` element carries no `lock="user"`. Measured against the shipped 6,000-token ceiling, on stores of user locks of 150 characters plus corroborated beliefs of 2,000: 80 locks plus 20 such beliefs gave 82 `seen` ids against 80 elements, 2 dangling; 70 plus 30 gave 72 against 70; 60 plus 40 gave 62 against 61. A core-only control of 40 beliefs of 4,000 characters fits under the ceiling and gives 4 against 4, dangling nothing. Every one of those is 0 dangling with the pointer removal in place. `enforce_block_ceiling` now removes the pointer with the element rather than exempting a pointed-at element from the drop, because exempting it would let a size optimisation pin bytes in place — on a first prompt where every `<core>` entry is also a hit, that is the whole droppable set. A pointer to a belief rendered on an earlier turn is untouched: no element in this body carries its id.

  **The ceiling is a real limit, not an unreachable one.** An earlier revision of this entry and of the constant's docstring said it was "set well above the sum of the per-lane budgets so it does not fire on a healthy store". That was not measured and is false: on a session's first prompt, where the `<locked>` sub-block and the per-turn hits share one envelope, it first reports at 66 user locks of 150 characters and at 58 of 200 — re-derive with `uv run python scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --lock-chars 150 200`. Override with `AELFRICE_HOOK_BLOCK_CEILING`; only a literal `0` disables it, and a negative or unparseable value falls back to the default with a note rather than silently removing the backstop, which `-1` used to do.
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#first_trim_locks_150 = 66 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#first_trim_locks_200 = 58 -->

  **A dropped belief stays unexplored.** `search_for_prompt` writes one `feedback_history` row per hit tagged `source='hook'`, and that row is this codebase's exposure record: `store.exploration_pool` (#1176) draws beliefs that have none, so a row written for a belief the model never saw evicts it from the never-shown pool for good, and #1279's slot exists because a belief with no exposure can never earn its way into a pack. The row was written inside `_retrieve`, before the block was assembled and the ceiling had run. Measured on a store of 60 user locks of 150 characters and 20 hits of 412, one fire on shipped defaults: 3 elements dropped, all 3 collecting a row and a `last_retrieved_at` stamp, and the pool falling 20 to 14 with 3 of the 6 departures never rendered. The write now happens at the emit boundary against the emitted set — 63 rows, none unrendered, pool 20 to 17. It moves whole rather than in pieces, so `record_retrieval` keeps resolving one timestamp for the audit row and the `last_retrieved_at` mirror inside a single transaction, which is #1373's invariant. `_retrieve` no longer takes a `record_exposure` parameter: nothing visible from inside it decides whether the model saw a belief.

  **One accounting write stays upstream of the ceiling, and now says so.** The `exploration_events` row (#1279) is written where the draw is made, before the block is assembled, so on an over-ceiling block it can name a belief the model never saw: the drawn belief is appended to the tail of the pack and the per-turn tail is what the ceiling sheds first. Measured at the shipped ceiling on a 60-lock / 20-core / 12-hit store (`scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --exploration`), the emitted block was identical with the slot on and off at 5923 estimated tokens while the ledger recorded one draw and one displacement, 0 of which reached the model. The row is kept where it is — it is the replay record of a pack decision, and a ledger that skipped the fires whose draw was dropped would be missing exactly the fires an operator re-deriving a seed goes looking for — and `_substitute_exploration_slots`, `docs/user/CONFIG.md` and a wiring test now all state the asymmetry. It costs a coverage figure counted from `drawn_ids` alone, which over-counts; the join against `injection_events` that CONFIG.md already specifies is correct, because that write moved to the emit boundary here.
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#exploration_slot_block_tokens = 5923 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#exploration_slot_drawn_emitted = 0 -->

  **The exploration slot prices its displacement in the same currency.** #1279 substitutes a never-injected belief for enough of the non-locked tail to pay for itself, and computed both sides with `retrieval._belief_tokens` — the whole of a belief's content, which this lane stopped emitting when the per-belief cap landed. On a 35,012-character belief the lane's own `_ups_belief_line_cost` charges 314 tokens against that function's 8,766, so a drawn belief over the cap demanded displacement it would never occupy and a displaced one was credited with freeing budget already trimmed out of the block. Both sides now charge `_ups_belief_line_cost`.

  The UserPromptSubmit lane also charges the line it renders. It passes `hook._ups_belief_line_cost` to `retrieve()` as `belief_cost_fn`, the counterpart of `hook_search_tool._belief_line_cost` on the PreToolUse lane (#1526 item 4). Without it the packer charged the whole of a belief's content while the renderer emitted at most `BELIEF_CONTENT_CHAR_CAP` of it, so a 35,012-character belief cost 8,766 tokens against a budget of 1,500, was rejected outright, and the fire injected an **empty block** — the cap never ran, on the lane the cap was added for. The same belief renders a 1,280-character element and now costs 321, admitted and capped.

  Re-tuning `DEFAULT_HOOK_TOKEN_BUDGET` itself is still not attempted: that needs a retrieval-quality gate rather than a byte count, as that constant's docstring already flags. A capped belief keeps its id, so its full text stays reachable through `aelf search` or an anchored document.
- **The published-figure gate reads code with a block scanner and a run-length span matcher, so a marker near a backtick is no longer silently dropped ([#1556](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1556)).** `scripts/check_derived_figures.py` excludes code before it parses a marker, so a figure quoted in a shell transcript is not read as a published claim. It did that with a single regex — ``` ``.*?``|`[^`]*` ``` — which paired backticks across the whole document, with no notion of a block and no notion of run length. A run of three is odd: two backticks paired, and the third became an opener that blanked everything down to the next backtick anywhere below it. **`docs/user/PRIVACY.md` carries six delimiter lines and parsed 0 markers**, the count a page with no markers in it also reports. A marker that never parses is indistinguishable from a figure nobody annotated, so the gate reported success over an unguarded figure — the #1160 defect class, inside the gate built to stop it. Measured on this branch's tree, Markdown markers move from **112** to **116**, and the repository total from **197** to **201**; `docs/user/CONFIG.md` and `docs/user/PRIVACY.md` each move from **0** to **2**.

  **The repair is two rules, because either one alone fails a bar the other holds.** `code_block_spans` follows CommonMark's fenced-code rules — an opener of three or more backticks or tildes indented at most three spaces, a backtick opener's info string carrying no backtick, and a closer of the same character, at least as long, with nothing after it but whitespace. `code_span_spans` follows CommonMark's inline rule — a span opens on a maximal run of backticks and closes on the next run of exactly that length, within one blank-line-delimited paragraph — so a run with no equal-length partner is literal text rather than a dangling opener. Over the 300-document seeded corpus in `tests/test_code_span_scanner_1556.py`, the block scanner alone leaves **56** documents violating the acceptance bar and loses **9** prose markers the old rule parsed; the span matcher alone leaks **188** markers out of fenced blocks, which deletes the feature the masking exists for; together both counts are **0**. The live corpus cannot make that distinction — either half recovers the same four markers there — so the generated corpus is the instrument that separates them.

  **The acceptance bar is absolute, and a differential prices the change beside it.** The bar: a marker outside every fenced block, in a paragraph carrying no backtick, parses, at its own line. The old rule breaks that on **93** of the 300 generated documents and this one on none. The differential holds the new scanner to a superset of what the old one parsed, and the one exception is enumerated rather than waived: where the old rule leaked a marker *out of* a real code block, this one correctly hides it.

  **Two declared divergences from CommonMark, and one of them now announces itself.** An unterminated opener masks nothing, where CommonMark runs the block to the end of its container — blanking to the end of the document is the exact shape of the defect being fixed. No file in the scanned corpus carries one today, so the two readings agree here, and `unterminated_fence` raises an advisory naming the line whenever a file grows one, because a silent divergence is how a gate goes quiet. The advisory quotes the closer length and the indent width the scanner itself enforces, and a test builds each remedy out of the message that was printed, so advice that would not clear the warning is red. Indented code blocks are not masked, because four-space indentation is the body of every Python function in a corpus that includes `src/`. Not masked either, and not masked before: HTML blocks, link reference definitions, and fences nested inside a blockquote or list item.

  Masking is now a whole-document decision made once, in `read_scannable`. It was not one before: a block containing a blank line lands in two `split_entries` entries, and an entry-level scanner holding an opener with no closer has to guess. Entry boundaries are still read off the raw file, because a blanked block is a run of whitespace-only lines and splitting the masked text would cut a page in two between a figure and the marker that names it. `split_lines` replaces `str.splitlines()` throughout, so the masked line list and the raw one agree on what a line is: `splitlines()` breaks on eight characters that masking turns into spaces, and one of them inside a block slid every entry after it and made the gate hard-fail a correct page at the wrong line.

  `docs/user/PRIVACY.md` can now publish the two budgets it names. The per-turn hook budget and the library retrieval budget gained markers on that page, and `benchmarks/published_constants.py` gained the two keys that re-derive them from the shipped constants, so a value edited in one place and not the other is red.
- **`aelf lock --reference` now bounds a lock on a session's first prompt too ([#1558](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1558)).** The reference tier exists so long-form locked material costs one `ref <id>: "<topic>"` line and is read on demand ([#1016-B](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1016)). Two lanes render a lock and only one honoured the tier: `_split_belief_lines` diverts a reference lock to `retrieval.lock_manifest_line`, while the `<locked>` loop of `_build_session_start_subblock` rendered every row through `b.content` with no `is_reference_lock` branch anywhere in it. The third `<belief>` renderer, `<core>`'s `_core_belief_line`, has no such branch either and is not a third instance of the defect: it is never handed a lock, because `_build_session_start_subblock` filters every id in `store.list_locked_beliefs()` out of `core_candidates`. `<core>` is covered by **exclusion**, `_split_belief_lines` by **diversion**, and the `<locked>` loop had neither. That sub-block is what a session's **first** prompt carries, so the tier was a measured no-op exactly where a lock-only store overruns the block ceiling, and on the retrieval branch the envelope carried the full text **and** a `ref` pointer to that same text a few lines below it. Measured on one 30,026-character lock: the gate-skip branch of a first prompt goes from 7700 estimated tokens at either tier to 7700 frozen against 273 reference, and the retrieval branch from 7796 at either tier to 7796 against 273. Turn two (256 against 7683) and the `session_start` baseline (233 against 7660) already bounded and are unchanged. Re-derive the table with `uv run python scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py --reference-tier`; `--emit-figures` publishes the same numbers so CI re-runs them.
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_gate_skip_first_frozen = 7700 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_gate_skip_first_reference = 273 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_retrieval_first_frozen = 7796 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_retrieval_first_reference = 273 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_turn_two_frozen = 7683 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_turn_two_reference = 256 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_session_start_frozen = 7660 -->
  <!-- derived: scripts/measure_block_ceiling.py#ref_lock_30026_session_start_reference = 233 -->

  **The row is diverted, not the element's content swapped.** A reference lock leaves the `<belief>` loop entirely and becomes one entry in an `<aelfrice-locks-manifest>` block, through the same `_manifest_block_lines` wrapper every other manifest goes through. The alternative — keeping the element and putting the manifest line in it — would have put the id into `_ids_rendered_verbatim_in`, whose contract is that the full text is in this context window. It is not, so [#1547](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1547)'s dedupe would have downgraded the per-turn copy to a `seen` pointer claiming text nobody sent, and the [#1382](https://github.com/robotrocketscience/aelfrice/issues/1382) ledger would have recorded an exposure that never happened. Diverting the row keeps both of those true with no second edit.

  **The duplicate pointer is dropped, and it was not free.** With the sub-block emitting its own `ref` line, the per-turn pack reached the same id through the same `_renders_as_manifest` predicate and pointed at it again, so the retrieval envelope carried the identical line twice — #1547's duplicate in its manifest form. `_drop_duplicate_ref_lines` removes the second one by id, after the split rather than before it, so the hit stays in `hits` and the ledger, the audit row and the exposure writes are unchanged. `seen` lines are not matched and pass through: they name an element rendered verbatim in this window, which is a different claim with its own drop accounting in `enforce_block_ceiling`.

  **One manifest wrapper per envelope.** The sub-block wraps its own entries, which is right when it is read alone and redundant once it is embedded, so a first-prompt envelope could open `<aelfrice-locks-manifest>` twice and repeat its 233-character framing note — on the very write this entry exists to shrink. It happens whenever the sub-block has a reference lock and the per-turn pack has a pointer of its own, which a store of one frozen and one reference lock reproduces: two wrappers before, one after. `_lift_manifest_block` cuts the sub-block's wrapper out and its entries lead the per-turn entries under the envelope's single wrapper. It extracts on the tag constants, which belief content cannot forge — `_escape_for_hook_block` entity-escapes every angle bracket before content reaches a rendered block.

  That also settles the dedupe's missing element-span guard. `enforce_block_ceiling` has to ignore `seen` matches that fall inside a `<belief>` element, because escaped content keeps its newlines and can hold a line shaped like a manifest entry; `_drop_duplicate_ref_lines` needs no such guard because it is no longer handed a rendered block at all, only the lifted entries.
